  ii
                                          Foreword

As the first cohort to embark on the Nine Year Continuous Basic Education at secondary level, we are
pleased to offer you a new series of Grade 8 textbooks. These textbooks have been designed in line
with the National Curriculum Framework (NCF) and syllabi for Grades 7, 8 and 9 − documents that
offer a comprehensive notion of learning and teaching with regard to each subject area. These may be
consulted on the MIE website, www.mie.ac.mu.

This set of textbooks builds upon the competencies acquired at Grade 7 level. As per the philosophy
propounded by the NCF, the content and pedagogical approach as well as the activities allow for an
incremental and continuous improvement of the learners’ cognitive skills. The content is contextual
and based on the needs of our learners which is constructed on a spiral basis. The comments and
suggestions of a variety of stakeholders have been kept in mind. We are especially appreciative of those
made by educators who have been part of our validation panels, and whose suggestions emanate from
long standing experience and practice in the field.

The production of this series of textbooks continue to be a challenge for the writers who have ensured
that the learners’ experience is further enriched. I would therefore wish to thank all those who have
been part of the whole process, for the time they devoted to it and their ability to maintaining the high
standard and quality of the textbooks, while ensuring that the objectives of the National Curriculum
Framework are skilfully translated in the content.

Every endeavour involves a number of dedicated, hardworking and able staff whose contribution
needs to be acknowledged. Professor Vassen Naeck, Head Curriculum Implementation and Textbook
Development and Evaluation, who has provided the guidance with respect to the objectives of the NCF,
while ascertaining that the instruction designs are appropriate for the age group being targeted. I also
acknowledge the efforts of the graphic artists for putting in much hard work to ensure the quality of the
MIE’s publications. My thanks go equally to the support staff who ensured that everyone receives the
necessary support and work environment which is conducive to a creative endeavour.

I am thankful to all those who provided the support and to the Ministry of Education, Human Resources,
Tertiary Education and Scientific Research for giving us the opportunity to be part of the whole reform
process.

Wishing plenty of joy and success to all students.



Dr O Nath Varma
Director
Mauritius Institute of Education




                                                                                                     iii
                                           Preface


The Grade 8 Mathematics textbook is intended to facilitate the teaching and learning of Mathematics
as put forward by the National Curriculum Framework (2017 – Mathematics). The focus is on the five
main content areas, namely Numbers, Algebra, Measures, Geometry and Probability & Statistics.


As with the Grade 7 Mathematics textbook, this textbook has been designed to encourage sense-
making and reasoning, which is the central focus of the Mathematics curriculum framework Grades 7
to 9. Thus, throughout the textbook, students will come across various activities to engage them in the
learning of Mathematics. Indeed, Mathematics is considered as one of the most important subjects
and it has many functional uses. Students encounter mathematics every day inside or outside the
classroom. Therefore, in line with the key components of the Mathematics Curriculum Framework
(Grades 7-9), real-life examples for the different concepts to be studied in Grade 8 have been made
prominent within the textbook. The aim is to arouse learners’ interest in the subject and to empower
them to consider mathematics as an important and powerful tool in their everyday life.


The panel has placed much emphasis on writing the textbook in a learner-friendly manner. Before
introducing new concepts or extending existing ones, concepts at primary and Grade 7 levels are
reviewed and consolidated. Different activities have been included to cater for different types of
learners. In addition, links to websites have been included as well as the use of the mathematical
software Geogebra so as to encourage students to explore, investigate and solve mathematical
problems.


As an added support, additional mathematics problems have been included at the end of the book for
students to practise and enhance their problem-solving skills. Furthermore, answers to the different
questions are provided on the MIE website, www.mie.ac.mu


We hope that this textbook will be a valuable resource for both learners and educators and that it
facilitates the teaching and learning process to achieve the aims of the Mathematics curriculum, thus
empowering students to become lifelong learners and function as 21st century global citizens.



The Mathematics Panel

Chapter 1 - Number Sequences

Introduction
In Grade 7, you learnt about sequences involving odd, even, square and triangular numbers.
You also learnt about sequences following a particular rule or sequences involving figures.

A number sequence is a list of numbers that follow a particular rule. In this chapter, you will
learn more about number sequences, particularly about Fibonacci sequence and sequence of
ordered pairs.

Sequence of ordered pairs                                                               Note: An ordered pair
                                                                                        is always written in the
Consider the following sequence:                                                        form (x, y).

       (1, 3), (2, 5), (3, 7), (4, 9), . . .

This type of sequence is known as a sequence of ordered pairs.
What do you observe?

Consider the first numbers in each pair. We obtain 1, 2, 3, 4, ...
It is a sequence with the rule ‘add 1 to a particular term to get the next term’.

Similarly, we consider all the second numbers in each pair. We obtain 3, 5, 7, 9, ...
It is a sequence with the rule ‘add 2 to each term to get the next term'.

Chapter 1 - Number Sequences

                                                                                                       5
Chapter 1 - Number Sequences

Investigate
Mr Jones offers his son, Jamie, two options to earn some pocket money during the holidays. On
one hand, Jamie can help paint the house and he will receive Rs 100 on Day 1, Rs 110 on Day 2,
Rs 120 on Day 3, etc. On the other hand, Jamie can water the plants as well as weed the garden
for which he will receive Rs 2 on Day 1, Rs 4 on Day 2, Rs 8 on Day 3, etc.

School will resume after Day 10. Which option should Jamie choose to earn more pocket money
during the ten days? Why?


  Summary
      • A number sequence is a list of numbers which follows a certain rule.
      • A Fibonacci sequence is a special type of sequence where a term can be obtained by
        adding its preceding two terms.
      • We can construct a Fibonacci sequence if we know any two consecutive terms.
      • In a sequence of ordered pairs, all the first numbers in the ordered pairs form a
        sequence. All the second numbers in the ordered pairs also form a sequence.

Example 1

Simplify:
(a) (ab)3 		              (b) (xy2)4		

Solution

(a) (ab)3 = a3 × b3 = a3 b3

(b) (xy2)4 = x4 × (y2)4 = x4y8

Example 2

Simplify the following as far as possible:
(a) (2a)2		           (b) (3x2y3)3		            (c) 5(10b)4

Solution

(a) (2a)2 = 22 × a2 = 4a2

(b) (3x2y3)3 = 33 × (x2)3 × (y3)3 = 27x6y9

(c) 5(10b)4= 5 × 104 × b4 = 5 ×10 000 × b4 = 50 000b4

Fractional indices
                                                                     1
Unit fractional indices in the form                                    where p is a positive integer and p ≠ 0
                                                                     p
A unit fractional index is a number with a fraction in the index where the numerator is one
                                                                                           1
and the denominator is a positive integer in the form a n.
                               1     1       1
Examples are 4 2 , 8 3 , 814 .

Square numbers and square roots
         RECALL

The square of a number is a number multiplied by itself. The square of 3 or “3 squared” means
32 and is equal to 9 (32 = 3 × 3 = 9).

We use the symbol √             to denote the square root of a number.                CHECK THIS LINK

problems than to use radicals ( √ ).

Solution

(b) (-4)3 = -4 × -4 × -4 = -64

(c) (0.6)3 = 0.6 × 0.6 × 0.6 = 0.216

Solving problems involving perfect squares and square roots
1. Square root of the product of two numbers

Consider the information in the table below.

              Number expressed
               as the product of
 Number      two square numbers         √ab       √(a × b)     √a      √b             √a × √b
                      (a × b)

    100               25 × 4          √100 = 10   √25 × 4    √25 = 5 √4 = 2 √25 × √4 = 5 × 2 =10
    144               36 × 4
    729               81 × 9
    625               25 × 25


Copy and complete the above table.                                            Note: A number is said to
Observe the 3rd, 4th and 7th columns. What do you notice?                     be a perfect square when
                                                                              its square root is an integer.

		 √(a × b) = √a × √b

Example 1

Find the value of:
(a) √2 500 			                  (b)√810 000

Solution

√2 500 = √25 × 100  We write 2 500 as a product of two perfect square numbers
       = √25 × √100 We use the property √(a × b) = √a × √b
       = 5 × 10     We calculate the square roots of 25 and of 100
       = 50					

(b)√810 000 = √81 × 10 000
            = √81 × √10 000 = 9 × 100
            			             = 900

Example 2

Find the value of:
(a)√196		                (b) √1 764

Solution

(a) √196 = √4 × 49
        = √4 × √49 = 2 × 7
                   = 14

 18
                                                                                     Chapter 2 - Indices



(b) Here, we do not know which two square numbers can be multiplied to obtain 1 764.
    We, therefore, use the method of dividing 1 764 by its prime factors.

		             = 42

Example

Given that √3 = 1.732 and √30 = 5.477, find the value of:
(a) √300		              (b) √3 000 		          (c) √120

Solution
(a) √300 = √3 × 100 		         We write 300 as a product of 3 and a square number.
         = √3 × √100		         We use the property √(a × b) = √a × √b .

         = 1.732 ×10		         We use the given information that √3 = 1.732.		
         = 17.32
(b) √3 000 = √30 × 100
           = √30 × √100
           = 5.477 ×10
           = 54.77


(c)√120 = √30 × 4                     We express 120 in terms of the product of 2 numbers
                                      where one of the numbers is a perfect square number.
        = √30 × √4
        = 5.477 × 2
        = 10.954

      REAL NUMBERS                                                                                3
Learning
			      Objectives
By the end of this chapter, you should be able to:
• demonstrate an understanding of rational and irrational numbers.
• represent real numbers on a number line.
• compare and order real numbers.
• convert a fraction into a decimal to identify the recurring part of non-terminating decimals.
• approximate a given number to required number of decimal places.

2. Set of Whole numbers

The set of Whole numbers consists of the set of Natural numbers and zero.
Set of whole numbers = {0, 1, 2, 3, …}. The set of whole numbers is also infini e.


                                                                        Whole numbers

                                0         1, 2, 3, …               Natural numbers




We say that the set of Natural numbers is a subset of the set of Whole numbers.

So, 1 = 3 and 1 = 5 .

Terminating and non-terminating decimals

Terminating decimals are decimals having a finite number of digits after the decimal point.
Examples are 0.5, 0.134, 0.25, 0.5689

               5
Now consider     .
               7
            5
To convert into a decimal, we use long division as we cannot express it into an equivalent
            7
fraction with denominator 10, 100, etc.

                                 0.7 1 4 2 8 . . .
                            75.0 0 0 0 0
                            -4 9
                               1 0
                              - 7
                                 3 0
                               - 2 8
                                    2 0
                                  - 1 4
                                       6 0
                                     - 5 6
                                         4
                           		            .
                           		            .
                                         .

0.71428… is called a non-terminating decimal as it contains an infinite number of digits
after the decimal point.

A decimal number that contains a pattern of digits which repeats indefini ely after the
decimal point is called a recurring decimal or a repeating decimal.

The non-terminating decimal can be written in ordinary notation or in dot notation.

We use a dot to indicate recurring decimals. For example,
    2
       = 0.666666…. (ordinary notation)
    3
       = 0.6 (dot notation)
Here the digit 6 is recurring. A dot is placed over the digit 6.

     8
        = 0.72727272…. = 0.72
     11

Here the digits 7 and 2 are recurring. A dot is placed over each of the 2 digits.

    7
       = 0.46666666 = 0.46
    15
Here only the digit 6 is recurring. So a dot is placed over 6 only.

       = 0.3409090909… = 0.3409                                             Note: Any repeating decimal
   44

Here the digits 0 and 9 are recurring. Dots are therefore placed
over 0 and 9.

Set of Irrational numbers
                                                                    p
Irrational numbers are numbers that cannot be expressed in the form q (q ≠ 0)
(including non-repeating and non-terminating decimals).
                                                                                                           DID YOU KNOW
Example: π = 3.14159… , √2 = 1.412… , √3 = 1.732…, and so on.

                                                                                              Hippasus, an Italian philosopher,
Caution: √9 is NOT an irrational number as 9 is a square number.                              discovered the set of irrational

Estimation
Throughout our daily life, we use estimations as we do not need exact or precise answers.
Reasonable estimates can be enough to solve the problem. For example, we can estimate
the number of people attending a football match or we can estimate the distance between
Vacoas and Beau-Bassin. Estimates can be found by rounding numbers to the nearest whole
number, to the nearest 10, 100, 1000 and so on.

(a) Rounding to the nearest ten
Example 1

Round 32 to the nearest tens.

         Tens              Units
           3                2                   30           32                            40

32 is nearer to 30 (3 tens) than to 40 (4 tens).
32 is approximated to 30 (correct to the nearest ten)
      If the unit value is either 0, 1, 2, 3 or 4, then the tens value remains as it is.


 36
                                                                              Chapter 3 - Real Numbers
Example 2
Round 738 to the nearest ten.

    730                               738     740

738 is nearer to 740 than to 730.
738 is approximated to 740 (correct to the nearest ten)
       If the unit value is either 5, 6, 7, 8 or 9, then the tens value is rounded up
       (i.e. add one to the tens value).

(b) Rounding to the nearest hundred
Example 3
Round 738 to the nearest hundred.

    700             738                       800
738 is nearer to 700 (7 hundreds) than to 800 (8 hundreds).
738 is approximated to 700
        If the tens value is either 0, 1, 2, 3 or 4, then the hundreds value remains as it is.

Example 4

Round 460 correct to the nearest hundred.
460 is approximated to 500 (correct to the nearest hundred)
        If the tens value is either 5, 6, 7, 8 or 9, then the hundreds value is rounded up.

Decimal place
A decimal place is the position of a digit after a decimal point.
Example:
			                              1st decimal place
			                    2.65
				                             2nd decimal place
2.65 is written correct to 2 decimal places or 2 d.p. as there are 2 digits after the decimal point.
Example:
     Number          Number of digits after the decimal point Written correct to
      0.0732                                4                                4 d.p.
     2568.67                                2                                2 d.p.
        15.0                                1                                1 d.p.
  1.5234578912                             10                               10 d.p.
                                                                                                  37

 Rounding decimals to the nearest whole number
 A decimal number can be rounded to the nearest whole number.
 Example 1
 Round to the nearest whole number.
 (a) 3.2

            3       3.2                         4

   3.2 is nearer to the whole number 3 than 4. 3.2 is approximated to 3 correct to the nearest
   whole number.
 (b) 3.75

                3                       3.75        4
   Note that 3.75 is nearer to 4 than to 3.
   3.75 is approximated to 4 correct to the nearest whole number.

 Rule: If the number at the 1st decimal place is either 0, 1, 2, 3 or 4, then the whole number
 remains as it is.
 (c) 14.38
     14.38 is approximated to 14 correct to the nearest whole number.


 (d) 0.157 is approximated to 0 correct to the nearest whole number.

 Rule: If the number at the 1st decimal place is either 5, 6, 7, 8 or 9, then the whole number is
 rounded up (i.e. add 1 to the whole number).

 (e) 124. 83
     124.83 is approximated to 125 correct to the nearest whole number.

 (f ) 2.5
      2.5 is approximated to 3 correct to the nearest whole number.

(g) 65.51       (h) 5646.879        (i) 199.9999      (j) 600.90

Rounding a number to the indicated decimal place.
We can round a number to the indicated decimal place. When rounding, we consider the
'critical digit'- which is the digit immediately after the rounding digit. For example, if we need
to round to 2 d.p., we consider the digit in the 3rd position after the decimal point. If the critical
digit is less than five, the rounding digit is not changed but if the critical digit is 5 or more, the
rounding digit is increased by one.

Example
1. Write 7.32 correct to 1 decimal place.

		                    1st decimal place

		7.32 = 7.3 (correct to the nearest 1 d.p.)

		                 If the 2nd decimal place is either 0, 1, 2, 3 or 4, the 1st decimal place remains as it is.

2. Write 45. 62397 correct to 3 decimal places.

			                         3rd decimal place

		45.62397 = 45.624 (correct to the nearest 3 d.p.)

			If the 4th decimal place is either 5, 6, 7, 8 or 9, the digit in the 3rd decimal place is
			                       increased by one (i.e. add one to the digit in the third decimal place).

   Summary
        Real Numbers
        The combination of both rational and irrational numbers is defined as the set of Real
        numbers and is denoted by .


        Natural numbers = { 1, 2, 3 … }
        Whole numbers = { 0, 1, 2, 3 … }
        Integers = { …, -3, -2, -1, 0, 1, 2, 3 … }
        Rational numbers= { …, -3, 1 , 3 , 7 , 6 , 4, 0.36, 0.128, 1.4 … }
                                                2     4   10    5
        Irrational numbers= { π, √2, √3… }

        Set of Rational Numbers,
                                                                           p
        Rational numbers are numbers that can be expressed in the form q , where p and q
        are both integers and q ≠ 0. The set of rational numbers can also be written as:
            p
           ={  , q ≠ 0, p, q  }
            q
        Example: 1 , 3 , 7 , 4, - 3, 0.36 …
                  2 4 10

        Set of Irrational Numbers
                                                                            p
        Irrational numbers are numbers that cannot be expressed in the form q , q ≠ 0
        (including non-repeating and non-terminating decimals).
        Example: π = 3.14159… √2 = 1.412… √3 = 1.732… and so on.

         Set of Real numbers


                                                                                  Irrational numbers

                                                               Whole numbers
                                         1, 2, 3, …             Natural numbers        π, √2, √3 …
                                     0

                                                                Integers
                                …, -3, -2, -1
                           1 , 3 , -0.36, 1.4 …                Rational numbers
                           2 4


        The set of Real numbers consists of both the Rational and Irrational numbers.

Rate
Rate is used to compare two quantities with different units. It
                                                                                           NOTE TO TEACHER
shows how a quantity changes with respect to another quantity.
For example, apples are on sale at 3 for Rs 10, tomatoes are sold                          Mention to students that in general,
at Rs 45 per kg or a worker earns Rs 100 per hour.                                         when rate is mentioned, it is referring
                                                                                           to unit rate unless otherwise stated.
The last two examples above are unit rates as we are comparing
a quantity to 1 unit of the other quantity (i.e. Rs 45 for 1 kg, Rs 100
for 1 hour).

 42
                                                                       Chapter 4 - Rate and Proportion

We use the term ‘per’ to describe a rate or denote rate by the symbol ‘/ ’ .

Example: If a printing machine prints 40 pages in 1 minute, we say that the rate of printing is
40 pages per minute.
We write it as 40 pages/min.

Example 1
A bus covers a distance of 10 km using 8 litres of fuel. Calculate the rate at which the bus
consumes fuel.

Solution
8 L are used to cover 10 km
                     10
1 L is used to cover    km =1.25 km
                      8
Hence, the rate at which the bus consumes fuel is 1.25 km/L.

Example 2
Jessma, a clerk typist, can type 150 words in 3 minutes.
(a) Find her typing rate.
(b) Find how long she will take to type a document of 2 800 words.

Solution
(a) 3 minutes		       150 words
                      150
   1 minute		              words = 50 words
                       3
   Thus, her typing rate is 50 words per minute or 50 words/min.


(b) Typing rate = 50 words/min
       50 words            1 minute

                           1
       1 word		                minute
                           50
                                1
       2 800 words		         (    × 2 800) minutes = 56 minutes.
                               50
       She will take 56 minutes to type 2 800 words.

Compound units

Since rates involve comparison of quantities in different units,
we say that they have compound units.

A compound unit is one that has two or more different types of
measurement, for e.g. km/h, Rs/kg.




 44
                                                                         Chapter 4 - Rate and Proportion

Conversion of compound units
Example 1
Convert Rs 60/kg to cents/g.
Solution
1 kg costs Rs 60

1000 g cost (60 ×100) cents 		We first convert kg into g and Rs into cents.

1 g costs 60 × 100 cents = 6 cents 		          We then find the unit rate for 1 g.
           1 000
So Rs 60/kg is 6 cents/g.

In 1 hour, the car travels 97.2 km                                                             RECALL

                        97.2 × 1 000 m = 27 m

                            3 600                                                    1 min = 60 s
The speed of the car is 27 m/s

Proportion

We use the concept of proportion to solve problems involving                               DID YOU KNOW
ratios. In this section, you will learn how to solve problems
involving scales and inverse proportions.                                       One of the first nown maps of
                                                                                Mauritius was published in Amsterdam
Scales in practical situations                                                  in 1659.

Engineers, architects and sportsmen amongst others use
scales. Scale drawings and models are essential as they take
into consideration the actual measurements on the ground.
Architects and engineers make use of scale drawings to design
buildings and bridges.                                                             Amsterdam : Juan Blaeu, 1659,“I.
                                                                                   Mauritius (Estrecho de Nassao)”




 46
                                                                    Chapter 4 - Rate and Proportion


Scale drawings are used to provide accurate information about objects which have dimensions
that are too large or too small to be shown on a page. For example, suppose that we need to
represent Port Louis and Curepipe on a map.

The distance between the two places is 28 km. It will be very difficult to represent 28 km on
paper! We, therefore, use scale drawings to represent such a distance on a map.

A scale is usually expressed as a ratio.
Examples: Length of drawing: Actual length of object
            Distance on map: Actual distance
            Height of model building: Height of actual building

If the scale of a plan or on a map is 1: 100 000, it means that the real distance or measurement
is 100 000 times as long as it is on the plan or map.
This means that 1 cm on the map represents an actual distance of 100 000 cm.
Thus the scale 1: n means that 1 cm on map represents an actual distance of n cm.



Investigate
As part of a project, your Grade 8 class has decided to help the school with designing the plan
for an extension to the school. This plan will be used by the school if it is approved.

The extension to the school will include the following:
1. A store (4 m by 3 m)
2. A science laboratory (8 m by 6 m)
3. 2 classrooms (6 m by 4 m)

Represent this plan on an A4 sheet of paper. Explain how you take into consideration the
dimensions provided.

Tejasvi makes a model of a ship using a scale of 1: 40. The model ship has a length of 1.2 m.
Find the actual length of the ship in metres.

Solution

1 cm on the model represents an actual distance of 40 cm
1.2 m = 120 cm on the model represent (120 x 40) cm = 4 800 cm

Indirect (Inverse) proportion
Recall: Two quantities are said to be in direct proportion if when one quantity increases, the
other quantity increases at the same rate.

Two quantities are said to be inversely proportional if when one quantity increases, the other
quantity decreases at the same rate.

Consider the following example:
A drone covers a distance of 10 km at a speed of 10 km/h.
The table below shows the time taken by the drone to cover the same
distance if the speed was 20 km/h, 30 km/h, 40 km/h or 50 km/h.

           Speed of drone (km/h)                  10         20         30         40          50
                                                              1          1           1          1
   Time taken to cover 10 km (in hours)            1
                                                              2          3           4          5

We note that as the speed of the drone increases, the time taken to cover the same distance of 10
km decreases in proportion.

Chapter 4 - Rate and Proportion

Example 1
                                                                                                                               FIND OUT
20 workers can build a tower in 8 days. How long will it take 16
                                                                                                                   In many problems involving
workers to build the same tower given that they work at the same rate?
                                                                                                                   inverse proportion, it is
Solution                                                                                                           mentioned that workers
                                                                                                                   work at the same rate.
20 workers take 8 days to build a tower                                                                            Why is this so?
1 worker will take (8 × 20) days = 160 days
                      160                                                                                       NOTE TO TEACHER
16 workers will take       days = 10 days
                       16
Thus, the 16 workers will take 10 days to build a tower.                                                          Explain to pupils why we need
                                                                                                                  to multiply by 20 for 1 worker
                                                                                                                  and not divide by 20.
Example 2
Fifteen men take 40 days to build a house. How long will 25 men take to build the same
house, given that they work at the same rate?
Solution

15 men take 40 days
1 man will take (15 × 40) days = 600 days
                  600
25 men will take       days = 24 days
                   25
Thus, 25 men will take 24 days to build the house.

   Summary
      •   Rate is a comparison of two quantities with different units.
      •   We use the term ‘per’ or the symbol ‘/ ’ to denote rate.
      •   A scale of 1: n means that 1 cm on map represents an actual distance of n cm.
      •   Two quantities are said to be inversely proportional, if an increase in one quantity
          leads to a decrease in the other quantity at the same rate.

     PERSONAL AND HOUSEHOLD FINANCE
                                                                                                                 5
Learning
			      Objectives
By the end of this chapter, you should be able to:
• perform operations involving percentage.
• solve problems involving percentage increase/decrease.

Percentage increase
Suppose that there is an increase of 10% in the salary of a person. This means that for every
Rs 100 in the initial salary, there is an increase of Rs 10. Thus, instead of getting Rs 100, the
person gets Rs 110.
                                                                               100 %
     0%
                                         Before


                                                                                             10 %
                                                                                             more
                                             After




Example
The price of a book is Rs 80. It increases by 10%. What is the new price?

Solution

Method 1                                                       Method 2
Original price = Rs 80.                                        Original price = 100%
Percentage increase = 10%.                                     Percentage increase = 10%.
                         10                                    New value, in percentage = 100% + 10%
Value of the increase =      × Rs 80 = Rs 8.
                        100                                                              = 110%
New price = (Rs 80 + Rs 8) = Rs 88.                                         110
                                                               New price =      × Rs 80 = Rs 88.
                                                                            100
                                                                Rs 8
        In rupees   Rs 0                                      Rs 80 Rs 88
        In percentage 0 %                                     100 % 110 %
                                                                 10 %

Percentage decrease

Suppose that there is a decrease of 20 % in the pocket money of a student. This means that for
every Rs 100 in the initial pocket money, there is a decrease of Rs 20. Thus, instead of getting Rs 100
as pocket money, the student gets Rs 80.
                                                                                       100 %
          0%
                                                     Before



                                                      After




                                                                                  20% less

Decrease 60 kg by 20%.

Solution

Method 1                                        Method 2
Original value = 60 kg                          Original value = 100%
Percentage decrease = 20%.
                                                Percentage decrease = 20%.
                          20

                         100
                       = 12 kg.                                           = 80%
                                                                    80
New value = (60 - 12) = 48 kg                   New value, in kg =     × 60 kg = 48 kg.
                                                                   100
                                                    12 kg
        In kilogram   0 kg                       48 kg   60 kg
        In percentage 0 %                         80 % 100 %
                                                     20 %



We can deduce the following
For an increase: New value = Old value + Increase
For a decrease: New value = Old value – Decrease

Percentage Profit and oss
The cost price or buying price is the price at which an article is bought.
The selling price is the price at which an article is sold.
When the selling price is greater than the cost price, we say that a profi is made.

			            Profit = elling Price – Cost Price

If the selling price is lower than the cost price, we say that a loss is made.

			            Loss = Cost Price – Selling Price

Example
Terry bought a phone at Rs 8 000 and sold it at Rs 10 000.
(a) Find the profit that Terry made.
(b) Find the profit made as a percentage of the cost price.

Solution

Buying price = Rs 8 000.
Selling price = Rs 10 000.
Since the selling price is greater than the buying price, Terry has made a profit.
Terry’s profit = Selling Price – Buying Price
             = Rs 10 000 – Rs 8 000
             = Rs 2 000.

Method 1                                               Method 2
                       Profit
Percentage Profit = 		          × 100%        or
                     Cost Price                        Rs 8 000 100%
                     2 000                                        100
                 =         × 100% = 25%                Rs 1			%
                     8 000                                       8 000
                                                                   100
                                                       Rs 2 000 (       x 2 000) %
                                                                  8 000
                                                       		= 25%




            We can express the profit or loss in erms of percentage as follows:

                                  Profi
            Percentage Profit 		           × 100 %
                                Cost Price
                                 Loss
            Percentage Loss = 		          × 100 %
                               Cost Price

Discount




A discount is a reduction or deduction in the actual price of an article.

Suppose that the actual price of a shirt is Rs 800. This actual price is also known as the marked
price of the shirt. For instance, during end-of-year sales, a shop reduces the price of the shirt by
Rs 200. This amount of Rs 200 is known as a discount.

Hence, the customer is paying Rs 600 (Rs 800 – Rs 200) for the shirt. The amount (Rs 600) that
the customer pays is the sale price.

Thus, we have

                                Discount = Marked Price – Sale Price


To find the percentage discount:                                                   CHECK THIS LINK
Discount = Rs (800 – 600) = Rs 200

                  200
Rs 200		              × 100% = 25%                                       percent_shopping.html.
                  800
Hence,

                                                  Discount
			                        Percentage Discount = 		           × 100%
                                                 Marked Price

A commission is a fee paid as incentive, in particular, to salespersons to encourage them to
increase their sales. Commissions are usually given in terms of percentage, for example, 2% of
sales.



Example 1

How much commission did he receive?
Solution

Selling price of house = Rs 2 500 000

Commission received by agent = 3% of Rs 2 500 000
                                 3
				                         =      x Rs 2 500 000
                                100
  				                       = Rs 75 000

Simple Interest
Simple interest is usually earned on money deposited at the bank or paid when money is
borrowed from the bank. The amount deposited or borrowed is known as the principal. Simple
interest can be earned monthly or yearly and is often given as a percentage, per annum, of the
principal.

The formula for calculating simple interest is given as follows:                         DID YOU KNOW

                                     PRT
				I =                                                                In Mauritius, the commercial banks
where                                100
                                                                       are presently offering an interest
I = simple interest paid or received                                   rate of around 1.5% per annum on
P = principal or the amount invested/borrowed                          deposit at bank.
R = rate of interest
T = time (in years)
If a customer borrows money from the bank, he will have to return the initial amount borrowed
(Principal) plus the interest. This is called the Amount.
                                                                               Note: p.a. stands for per annum
			                     Amount = Principal + Interest                          which means per year.
Example 1

Tania borrowed Rs 125 000 to open her shop. If she paid a simple interest rate of 6% per
annum (p.a.) for 3 years, find the interest that she needed to pay.

Solution

Principal = Rs 125 000          Rate = 6% p.a.          Time = 3 years
           PRT
Interest =
           100
        125 000 × 6 × 3
I = Rs
             100
I = Rs 22 500

She had to pay Rs 22 500 as interest.

       •   A commission is a fee paid as incentive, in particular, to salespersons to encourage
           them to increase their sales. Commissions are usually given in terms of percentage,
           for example, 2% of sales.

Inclined or slant line
An inclined or slant line is neither horizontal nor vertical. It rather slopes up or down from left
to right.

Chapter 6 - Coordinates

We observe that the equation of the line can be written as y = x as the values of x are equal to
the values of y. Other examples of equation of inclined lines: y = 2x, y = 3x + 1, y = x – 4,
y = –4x + 3, y = –3x.
                                                                                              Note: Only two points
      Drawing lines                                                                           are enough to draw a line.
      To draw a line for a given equation, consider the following steps.
      (i) Find the coordinates of the points on the line for a given set of values of x.
      (ii) Plot these points on a well-labelled Cartesian plane.
      (iii) Using a ruler, join all the points to form a straight line.
      (iv) Label the line by its equation.


Example 1

(a) Copy and complete the following table of values for the equation y = x + 2.

        x                 –1                      0                 1                 2                3
      y=x+2
      (x, y)
				
(b) Draw and label the line y = x + 2 .
Solution

(a) Since y = x + 2, when
    x = –1, y = (–1) + 2 = 1		                x = 0, y = 0 + 2 = 2		                x = 1, y = 1 + 2 = 3
    x = 2, y = 2 + 2 = 4			                   x = 3, y = 3 + 2 = 5

         x                –1                      0                 1                 2                3
      y=x+2               1                       2                 3                 4                5
       (x, y)         (–1, 1)             (0, 2)                  (1, 3)            (2, 4)           (3, 5)


(b)                                       y-axis

                                          6

                                          5

                                -2   -1               1   2   3      4

                                                                                                       Chapter 6 - Coordinates

Example 2

(a) Copy and complete the following table of values for the equation y = 3x – 1.

          x             –1                  0                        1                      2                  3
      y = 3x – 1
      (x, y)
				
(b) Draw and label the line y = 3x – 1 .

Solution

(a) Since y = 3x – 1, when
    x = –1, y = (3 × (–1)) – 1 = –3 – 1 = –4
    x = 0, y = (3 × 0) – 1 = 0 – 1 = –1
    x = 1, y = (3 × 1) – 1 = 3 – 1 = 2
    x = 2, y = (3 × 2) – 1 = 6 – 1 = 5
    x = 3, y = (3 × 3) – 1 = 9 – 1 = 8

          x            –1                    0                       1                      2                  3
      y = 3x – 1       –4                   –1                       2                      5                  8
        (x, y)       (–1, –4)          (0, –1)                     (1, 2)                 (2, 5)             (3, 8)

Example 1

(a) Copy and fill in the table of values for the equation y = –2x given below.		
(b) Draw and label the line y = –2x for x from –2 to 2.

        x                –2                     –1                            0            1                    2
    y = –2x
      (x, y)

Solution

(a) Since y = –2x, when
    x = –2, y = (–2 × –2) = 4
    x = –1, y = (–2 × –1) = 2
    x = 0, y = (–2 × 0) = 0
    x = 1, y = (–2 × 1) = –2
    x = 2, y = (–2 × 2) = –4

                                                                                                                           77
Chapter 6 - Coordinates




         x                  –2                  –1                        0                 1               2
      y = –2x               4                   2                         0                 –2              –4
       (x, y)             (–2, 4)             (–1, 2)                 (0, 0)              (1, –2)         (2, –4)

(b) We then join all the points to form a straight line and we label this line y = –2x.

(i) y = 2x

What do you observe?

The point where the lines meet is called the point of intersection.

Write down the coordinates of the point of intersection of these two lines.




Note: Not all straight lines intersect on a Cartesian plane. For example, two vertical lines will never intersect each other as they are
parallel. The same applies for two horizontal lines.


Finding the point of intersection of two straight lines graphically

Steps to find the point of intersection of two straight lines graphically:

Step 1: Draw the lines on the Cartesian plane.
Step 2: Locate the point where these two straight lines intersect.
Step 3: Write down the coordinates of the point of intersection.

Chapter 6 - Coordinates

Summary
   •   An inclined or slant line is neither horizontal nor vertical. It rather slopes up or
       down from left to right.
              Vertical line                  Horizontal line                   Inclined lines
                 y-axis                           y-axis                           y-axis            Line 1


                                                               y=k

                  0
                                x-axis             0
                                                                 x-axis             0
                                                                                                        x-axis




                                                                                            Line 2
                          x=h


                                                                                                        85
Chapter 6 - Coordinates



   • Examples of equations of inclined lines are: y = 2x, y = 3x + 1, y = x – 4, y = –4x + 3, y = –3x.
   • To draw an inclined or slant line for a given equation, you need to:
      (i) Find the coordinates of some points on the line for a given set of values of x.
      (ii) Plot these points on a well-labelled Cartesian plane.
     (iii) Join all the points using a ruler to form a straight line.
     (iv) Write down the equation of the line.
   • To find the point of intersection of two straight lines graphically, you should:
      (i) Plot the two given straight lines on a well-labelled Cartesian plane.
      (ii) Locate the point where these two straight lines intersect.
      (iii) Write down the coordinates of the point of intersection.

1. (a) Copy and complete the table of values for the equation y = x + 1 given below.

            x              –2             –1             0              1              2
        y=x+1              –1
          (x, y)        (–2, –1)
   				
  (b) Draw the graph of y = x + 1 and the line y = 3 on a well-labelled Cartesian plane.
  (c) Write the coordinates of the point of intersection of these two straight lines.

The Pythagoras Theorem
                                                                                               c
In any right-angled triangle, the square of the length of the              a
hypotenuse is equal to the sum of the squares of the lengths
of the other two sides.
                                                                                           b
The formula used for the Pythagoras theorem is:
       (hypotenuse)2 = sum of the squares of the lengths                   Caution:
		                     of the other two sides                              The Pythagoras theorem can
                                                                           only be applied to right-angled
                                                                           triangles.
       i.e.           c2 = a2 + b2

If three positive integers satisfy this formula, we say that                                FIND OUT
these 3 integers form the Pythagorean triple.
For example, 6, 8 and 10 form a Pythagorean triple.                               Can you find some xamples of
         62 + 82 = 36 + 64 = 100                                                  Pythagorean triples?
             102 = 100
         62 + 82 = 102

Investigate
Deborah is trapped in a fire on the third floor of a building, 12 m high. A team of firefighters
comes to rescue her. They have 2 ladders of length 13 m and 20 m. Which ladder should they
use to reach the 3rd floor if the ladder is placed 5 m away from the building?


Applications of Pythagoras theorem in Real-life situations

Example 1

A ladder leans against a wall of a house. If the length of the ladder is 5 m and the base of the
ladder is 3 m from the house, find the distance, d.

Solution

Hypotenuse = 5 m, sides: 3 m and d m
Using the Pythagoras theorem,
52 = 32 + d2
32 + d2 = 52                                                       5m
                                                                                      Distance, d
d2 = 52 – 32
d2 = 25 – 9                        5m                 d
d2 = 16                                                                 3m
d = √16
d= 4                                 3m
Distance, d = 4 m.



 92
                                                                    Chapter 7 - Pythagoras Theorem

Example 2

A football playground is a rectangle of width 90 metres and length 120 metres. The
coach asked the players to run from one corner to the other corner diagonally across the
playground. What distance did each player run?
                                                                      120 m b
Solution

Using the Pythagoras theorem,
                                                          a       c                     90 m
c2 = a 2 + b 2
                                      b = 120 m
c2 = 902 + 1202
c2 = 8 100 + 14 400
                          a = 90 m          c
c2 = 22 500
c = √22 500
c = √(225 ×100)
c = 15 × 10
c = 150
Hence, each player ran a distance of 150 m across the playground.

Example 3

A wall is being painted and a ladder is placed on the wall to enable the painter to paint
the wall. If the ladder reaches 4 m up the wall and rests 3 m away from the foot of the
wall, find the length of the ladder.

Solution

Using the Pythagoras theorem,
h2 = 32 + 42                                                                      h
   = 9 + 16 = 25                                                         4m
 h = √25
                                                                             3m
   =5
The length of the ladder is 5 m.                                        Foot of wall


Example 4

                                   24 cm



         12 cm                                                x



                                      25 cm

                   Chapter 8 - H.C.F. of Algebraic Terms and Factorisation of Algebraic Expressions

      FACTORISATION OF ALGEBRAIC EXPRESSIONS                                                           8
Learning
			      Objectives
By the end of this chapter, you should be able to:
• find the H.C.F. of numbers and of algebraic terms.
• determine common factors in an algebraic expression.
• factorise algebraic expressions having common factors.
• factorise an algebraic expression involving four terms by grouping.



H.C.F. of algebraic terms is used to factorise algebraic                 CHECK THAT YOU CAN:
expressions. Factorisation of algebraic expressions is useful
in solving algebraic equations. Scientists, engineers and               • Find H.C.F. of numbers.
                                                                        • Express numbers as product of their
architects often make use of algebraic expressions and                    prime factors (in expanded and index
equations in their work.                                                  form).
                                                                        • Use the distributive law over addition.
        RECALL
                                                                                      KEY TERMS
The Highest Common Factor (H.C.F.) of
numbers                                                                   •   Factors
                                                                          •   Prime factorisation
                                                                          •   Algebraic terms
The Highest Common Factor (H.C.F.) of two or more numbers                 •   H.C.F. (Highest common factor)
is the largest factor that is common to these numbers.                    •   Factorisation
                                                                          •   Common factors
                                                                          •   Compound factors

Example

Find the H.C.F. of 30, 45 and 90.

Solution

Using Prime Factorisation                                                     Note: You can also find the H.C.F. by
                                                                              using other methods that you have
                                                                              learnt in earlier grades.
2 30		 3 45                   2 90
3 15		 3 15                   3 45
5 5		  5 5                    3 15
  1		    1                    5 5
  				                           1
In expanded form:				                     In index
                                                 		  form:
                                          30 = 2 × 3 × 5                 Note: We select the common factors
30 = 2 × 3      ×5
                                                                         which have the smallest power and find
45 =      3×3×5                           45 =      32 × 5               their product.
90 = 2 × 3 × 3 × 5                        90 = 2 × 3 × 5
                                                      2


H.C.F. of 30, 45 and 90 = 3 × 5 = 15      H.C.F. of 30, 45 and 90 = 3 × 5 = 15

(a) 12 and 30			                   (b) 16 and 40			                   (c) 36 and 90

(j) 28, 42 and 56		                (k) 36, 45 and 81		                (l) 56, 84 and 98


Algebraic Terms and Expressions
An algebraic term is either a single number or a variable, or numbers and variables
multiplied together. For example: 2, 3, a, b, x, y, 2x, 3y, 5xy, 7mn, ...

The Highest Common Factor (H.C.F.) of Algebraic Terms
The H.C.F. of algebraic terms is the product of the common factors
(numerical and algebraic factors).
                                                                                    NOTE TO TEACHER
                                                                                    Explain the meaning of numerical
                                                                                    and algebraic factors.
Example 1

Find the H.C.F. of 6x2y3 and 10 x3y.

Solution                                                                                       Note: We select the
                                                                                               common factors of the 2
Method 1:					                                            Method 2:                            expressions which have
                                                                                               the smallest power and
6 x2y3 = 2 × 3 × x × x    ×y×y×y                          6 x2y3= 2 × 3 × x2 × y3              find their product.Here we
10x y = 2 × 5 × x × x × x × y
    3
                                                          10x3y= 2 × 5 × x3 × y                choose x2 and y.

H.C.F. of 6x2y3 and 10x3y = 2 × x × x × y                 H.C.F. of 6x2y3 and 10 x3y
			                       = 2x2y     		                   = 2x2y


Example 2

Find the H.C.F. of 2p3q2 and 3r2z.

Solution

                                                                                       Note: We include × 1 to both terms to
2p3q2 = 2 × p3 × q2 × 1
                                                                                       obtain a common factor.
3r2z = 3 × r2 × z × 1

H.C.F. of 2p3q2 and 3r2z = 1

   2 3

Factorisation of Algebraic Expressions
Example

Factorise each of the following:                                                               RECALL
(a) 2x + 8		           (b) 15 – 3y
(c) -11a + 66		        (d) -25b – 30                                               By the distributive law,
                                                                                           Expanding
Solution                                                                              a (b + c) = ab + ac

                                                                                           Factorising
(a) 2x + 8
      2x = 2 ×         x                                                           The reverse procedure of expanding
       8 =2×2×2                                                                    brackets is called factorisation.
                                                                                   To factorise an expression, we need to
    H.C.F = 2
                                                                                   take out the highest common factor
    Hence 2x + 8 = (2 x x) + (2 x 2 x 2)                                           and place it in front of the brackets.
    2x + 8 = 2 (x + 4)                          Remember: Always expand the        ab + ac = a × b + a × c
                                                brackets to check whether the      a is a common factor to ab and ac
                                                answers are correct.               So, ab + ac = a ( b + c )
(b) 15 – 3y
      15 = 3 × 5                                                                      Note: To factorise an expression,
      3y = 3     ×y                                                                   rewrite it as a product of factors.
    H.C.F = 3
 15 – 3y = 3 × 5 – 3 × y
         = 3 (5 – y)

(c) -11a + 66
       -11a =      -1 × 11 × a
         66 = -1 × -1 × 11 × 6
                                                                                      Note: 66 = 1 x 11 x 6
     H.C.F = -1 × 11 = -11                                                                     = -1 x -1 x 11 x 6
     Hence -11a + 66 = -1 × 11 × a + -1 × -1 × 11 × 6
                       = -11(a – 6)
or
-11a + 66 = -1 × 11 × a + 11 × 6
                                                                                      Note: -a + b = b - a
           = 11 (-a + 6)
           = 11 (6 – a)

(d) -25b – 30
    -25b = -1 × 5 × 5 × b
     – 30 = -1 × 5 × 6
-25b – 30 = -1 × 5 × 5 × b + -1 × 5 × 6
           = -1 × 5(5b + 6)
           = -5 (5b + 6)

Example

Factorise each of the following:
                                                                                        5 2    1
(a) x2 + 5x            (b) 4ab – 14a2		         (c) 3x2 – 12xy + 9x 		            (d)     x y – xy2
                                                                                        6      6
Solution

(a) x2 + 5x = x × x + 5 × x		
            = x (x + 5) where x is the common factor

(b) 4ab – 14a2 = 2a × 2b – 2a × 7a
               = 2a (2b – 7a) where 2a is the common factor

(c) 3x2 – 12xy + 9x = 3x × x – 3x × 4y + 3x × 3 		
                    = 3x (x – 4y + 3) where 3x is the common factor

 5 2    1     1          1
(d)x y – xy2 = xy × 5x – xy × y 			
 6      6     6          6
		          = xy (5x – y) where 1 xy is the common factor
              1
              6                 6

Compound Factors

A compound factor is a common factor consisting of more than one term.

Let’s consider the expression 5y (x + 2) – 3 (x + 2).
		5y (x + 2) = 5 × y × (x + 2) and 3 (x + 2) = 3 × (x + 2)
We observe that (x + 2) is a common factor to 5y (x + 2) and 3 (x + 2).
(x + 2) is also called a compound factor.                                                       Note: (5y – 3)(x + 2)
                                                                                                     = (x + 2)(5y – 3)
(x + 2) can be taken out when factorising the expression. So, we have:
		5y(x + 2) – 3(x + 2) = (5y – 3)(x + 2)

Example

Factorise:
(a) 4x(2x - 5) – 3(2x - 5)         (b) 2a(x + 2) + (x + 2)		          (c) 3x(y + 1) + 2y(y + 1) – 3(y + 1)

Solution

(a) 4x(2x - 5) – 3(2x - 5) = (2x - 5)(4x – 3)		              Take out (2x - 5), the compound factor

(b) 2a(x + 2) + (x + 2) = 2a(x + 2) + 1(x + 2)		 Note that (x + 2) can be written as 1(x + 2)
                        = (x + 2)(2a + 1)		 Take out (x + 2), the compound factor

(a) 3x(y + 1) + 2y(y + 1) – 3(y + 1)
             = (y + 1)(3x + 2y – 3)			 Take out (y + 1), the compound factor

Factorisation by Grouping

Consider the expression 2x + 2 + bx + b.

You will observe that there is no factor common to all four terms in the expression.

However, we can group the first two terms together and the last two terms together as follows:

			2x + 2 + bx + b
               The first g ouping has           The second grouping has
                a common factor 2                 a common factor b

Hence,
		2x + 2 + bx + b = (2x + 2) + (bx + b)                                        Group the terms.
				= 2(x + 1) + b(x + 1)                                                      Take the common factors 2 and b out.
				= (x + 1)(2 + b) 		                                                        Take the compound factor (x + 1) out.



               CHECK THIS LINK

    This link helps you to practise factorisation of algebraic expressions:
    www.transum.org/software/SW/Starter_of_the_day/Students/Factorising.asp?Level=1

Chapter 8 - H.C.F. of Algebraic Terms and Factorisation of Algebraic Expressions


Example

Factorise:
(a) ab + ac + 3b + 3c		            (b) 2m2 + 10m – bm – 5b		
(c) 3x2 – 6x – 4x + 8		            (d) mp – mq – 3p + 3q

Solution

(a) ab + ac + 3b + 3c = (ab + ac) + (3b + 3c) 		             Group the terms.
 		                   = a(b + c) + 3(b + c) 		               Take the common factors a and 3 out.
		                    = (b + c)(a + 3) 			                   Take the compound factor (b + c) out.

(b) 2m2 + 10m – bm – 5b = (2m2 + 10m) – (bm + 5b)            Note the change in sign inside the brackets.
			                     = 2m(m + 5) – b(m + 5)
 			                    = (m + 5)(2m – b)		                  Take the compound factor (m + 5) out.

(c) 3x2 – 6x – 4x + 8 = (3x2 – 6x) – (4x – 8)		              Note the change in sign inside the brackets. 		
		                    = 3x(x – 2) – 4(x – 2)		
  		                  = (x – 2)(3x – 4)

(d) mp – mq – 3p + 3q = (mp – mq) – (3p – 3q)                Note the change in sign inside the brackets.		
			= m(p – q) – 3(p – q)
			= (p – q)(m – 3)                                                                     Caution:
                                                                                        The sign changes at the
                                                                                        instant the grouping occurs
                                                                                        if we have a negative sign in
                                                                                        front of the bracket.

Example

Factorise:
(a) xy + 3 + x + 3y			(b) ax + by – ay – bx

Solution

(a) Here, there are no common terms to the first pair and no common terms to the second pair.
    We, therefore rearrange the terms as follows: xy + x + 3 + 3y
    Hence,
    xy + 3 + x + 3y = xy + x + 3 + 3y
		                 = (xy + x) + (3 + 3y)
                   = x(y + 1) + 3(1 + y)
		                 = x(y + 1) + 3(y + 1)				                as (1 + y) = (y + 1)
		                 = (y + 1)(x + 3)

(b) ax + by – ay – bx = ax – ay – bx + by
		                    = (ax – ay) – (bx – by)			
  		                  = a(x – y) – b(x – y)
		                    = (x – y)(a – b)




        EXERCISE 8.7

Factorise each of the following:
(a) a + 2 + ab + 2b			(b) x – 3 + ax – 3a			(c) 2ax – 2a2 + ac – cx		
(d) ab – 12 + 3a – 4b			(e) 2fh – gk – fk + 2gh			(f ) x2y + 4 – 4x2 – y

Summary
   1. The H.C.F. of two or more numbers is the largest factor that is common to these numbers.
   2. The H.C.F. of algebraic terms involve the product of common factors raised to the
      smallest power.
   3. Distributive law:
                       Expanding


   			            a (b + c) = ab + ac
                       Factorising
   4. To factorise an expression, we need to take out the highest common factor and
      place it in front of the brackets.
   		ab + ac = a × b + a × c = a (b + c)
                   –ab + ac = –a (b – c) or = a (–b + c)


                                                                                                         107
Chapter 8 - H.C.F. of Algebraic Terms and Factorisation of Algebraic Expressions


       5. Compound Factor
          A compound factor is a common factor consisting of more than one term.
          For example, (x + 2) is the compound factor in the expression 5y(x + 2) – 3(x + 2).
       6. Factorisation by grouping
          For example, for the expression 2x + 2 + bx + b, we group the first two terms together.
          Factorise:
       		            2x + 2 + bx + b = (2x + 2) + (bx + b)
                                     = 2(x + 1) + b(x + 1)
                                     = (x + 1)(2 + b) or (x + 1)(b + 2)

The Lowest Common Multiple (L.C.M.) of Algebraic Terms
The L.C.M. of algebraic terms is the product of each factor with the highest power which
occurs in the given expressions.

L.C.M. of algebraic terms is used to find common denominators of algebraic fractions.

Example 1

Find the L.C.M. of 4x2y and 6xy.

Solution
                                                                        Note: We select the common
                                                                        algebraic terms with the highest
4x2y = 2 × 2 ×     x2 × y = 22 ×     x2 × y 				
                                                                        power to obtain the L.C.M.
6xy = 2 ×      3× x ×y =2×3× x ×y
L.C.M. of 4x y and 6xy = 22 × 3 × x2 × y = 12x2y
            2




Example 2

Find the L.C.M. of a2 and b3.
Solution

a2 = a2
b3 = b3
L.C.M. of a2 and b3 = a2b3


Example 3

Find the L.C.M. of 2x2y, 3x3y and 4xy2.

Solution

2x2y = 2       × x2 × y
3x3y =      3 × x3 × y
4xy2 = 22 × x × y2
L.C.M. of 2x2y, 3x3y and 4xy2 = 22 × 3 × x3 × y2 = 12 x3y2

Simplifi ation of Algebraic Fractions

We simplify algebraic fractions in the same way as we simplify
numerical fractions, that is, we divide both the numerator and
denominator by the highest common factor.                                                CHECK THIS LINK

       8x                           10x3y                        35a2b3                   12cd2
  (a) 		                        (b) 			                      (c) 			                  (d) 			
      10x2y                          15x2                         25ab                     28d

Addition and Subtraction of Algebraic Fractions with numerical denominators

Multiplication and Division of algebraic fractions

Multiplication
We multiply two or more algebraic fractions the same way as we multiply numerical fractions.
We divide the numerator and denominator by any common factors before multiplying.

      Multiplication with numerical fractions               Multiplication with algebraic fractions
                       1      1
        2 15   2 3x5                                        2x2 15y 2 x x x x 3 x 5 x y 1
                                                                        1   1             1

         x   = 1x 1                                            x   = 1 1 x 1
        5 16   5 2x8                                        5yz 16x 5 x y x z 2 x 8 x x 1
               3                                                     3x
             =                                                     =
               8                                                     8z

       https://www.mathsisfun.com/algebra/fractions-algebra.html



Summary

   • The L.C.M. of two or more numbers is the smallest (least) multiple that is common
     to the numbers.

   • L.C.M. of algebraic terms = L.C.M. of the numerical coefficients × each factor raised
     to the highest power.

   • Simplifi ation of Algebraic Fractions
     We simplify algebraic fractions in the same way that we simplify numerical fractions,
     that is, we divide both the numerator and denominator by the highest common
     factor.

   • Addition and Subtraction of Algebraic Fractions with numerical denominators
     and algebraic denominators
     Addition and subtraction of algebraic fractions follow exactly the same rules as for
     numerical fractions.


120
                                                  Chapter 9 - L.C.M. of Algebraic Terms and Algebraic Fractions




    • Multiplication and Division of algebraic fractions
      Multiplication
      We multiply two or more algebraic fractions the same way we multiply numerical
      fractions. We divide the numerator and denominator by any common factors
      before multiplying.

         Division
         Dividing algebraic fractions is similar to dividing numerical fractions. We take
         the reciprocal of the second fraction and then multiply them.

Learning
			      Objectives
By the end of this chapter, you should be able to:
• draw and interpret pie charts.
• solve problems from information given on a pie chart.
• demonstrate an understanding of and calculate the mean, mode and median of a
  discrete set of raw data.

                                                                            DID YOU KNOW

Pie Charts
                                                              The name “pie” was given to pie chart
                                                              since each sector resembles a slice of
A pie chart or pie graph consists of a circle divided into    pie.
sectors such that each sector represents a proportion of
the whole statistical data.

It is very simple to use a pie chart but at times it may
not be appropriate when there are too many sectors.

Interpreting pie charts
Example 1                                                                  DID YOU KNOW

There are 720 spectators during a football match. The pie              History of Pie Charts
chart below represents the number of men, women and chil-
dren attending the match. Use the information on the pie
chart to answer the following questions.



                                                                   William Playfair (1759 – 1823)

                                                                William Playfair, a Scottish engineer,
                                                                was the first to use pie charts in 1801
                                                                to indicate the proportions of the
                                                                Turkish Empire in Asia, Europe and
                                                                Africa before 1789.




       Number of spectators attending a match
   (a) Calculate the angle x.
                                                                           Pie chart (1801)
   (b) What fraction of the spectators were men?
                                                                In 1858, Charles Joseph Minard, a
   (c) Find the number of women among the spectators.           French engineer, used pie charts on
                                                                a map to illustrate information about
                                                                cattle sent for consumption to Paris
Solution                                                        from other parts of France.

   (a) The sum of the angles in a pie chart (circle is 360°).
       x = 360° – (90° + 120°) = 360° – 210° = 150°

   (b) ‘Men’ is represented by an angle of 90°.
                            90    9 1
       Fraction of men =        =   =
                            360    36 4
  (c) ‘Women’ is represented by an angle of 120°.

      360°		          720 spectators
                      720
       1°             360 spectators
                          2
      1200 		         720 x 120 = 240 spectators
                      360 1
  Hence, the number of women = 240.

Chapter 10 - Statistics

Example 2

The pie chart below shows Gina’s expenses during her last holiday trip. Given that Gina spent
Rs 9 000 on parasailing, use the information on the pie chart to answer the following questions.




                                    Gina’s holiday trip expenses

    (a) How much did the souvenirs cost Gina?
    (b) How much money did she spend on air tickets?
    (c) What percentage did she spend on parasailing?
    (d) On what item did she spend Rs 5 000?

    (e) What is the ratio of the money she spent on hotel bills to that on food?
    (f) How much money did Gina spend in all?


Solution

    (a) The ‘Parasailing’ cost was Rs 9 000 and is represented by a sector with a right angle.

          ‘Souvenirs’ is represented by an angle of 40°. Therefore, using direct proportion

		                90°          Rs 9 000

                                  ⎛ 40!  100 ⎞
                  40°                             (         )
                               Rs ⎜ ! × 9 000 ⎟ = 40 ×100 = Rs 4 000
                                  ⎝ 90 1      ⎠

          Souvenirs cost Gina Rs 4 000.

    (b) ‘Air tickets’ is represented by an angle of 100°.

MEAN, MODE AND MEDIAN

Measures of Average in real life                                                           RECALL

Averages are an important tool to summarise a large set of data into
                                                                               In Grade 6, you learnt about
a single value. The main measures of average are the mean, mode                one type of average which
and median.                                                                    was the mean.

Raw data

Sammy recorded the number of goals scored by his class team in ten matches during the
second term as shown below.




                                                  3, 1, 0, 0, 2,
                                                  1, 0, 2, 1, 0.




The above information is called raw data.

Raw data also known as primary data is a set of observations which has not been sorted or
organised for use.
For example:
Raw data
8, 4, 1, 2, 5, 2, 9, 3, 7, 2, 6, 3
Sorted data (in ascending order)
1, 2, 2, 2, 3, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9


Mean                                                                         Part of the results

The mean is the average of two or more numbers. Your percentage
marks each term is the mean of all the marks you obtain in the
different subjects that you take. This gives a general idea how you
have performed in your assessment papers.

The mean of a set of raw data is obtained by dividing the sum of all the observations by the
total number of observations.
                                                 sum of all observations         Note: Observation refers
                                     Mean =                                      to the data collected.
                                              total number of observations


                                                                                                      137
Chapter 10 - Statistics

Example 2

Find the mean of 0.6, 1.5, 0.75, 0.6, 0.9, 0.45, 0.6, 0.3 and 0.6.

                   sum of all observations

Example 3

The mean of a group of 4 numbers is 20. A fifth number is added to this group and the mean
of the 5 numbers is 22. Find the fifth number.

Solution

 Mean of 4 numbers = 20				                             Mean of 5 numbers = 22


    20     20     20      20                                 22      22    22       22       22

 Sum of 4 numbers = 20 x 4 = 80. 			                     Sum of the 5 numbers = 22 x 5 = 110.

 The fifth number = (110 – 80) = 30.

Mode

The mode is the number which occurs most often. It is very appropriate for fashion issues. For
example, the picture below shows the different sports shoes found in an athletes’ changing
room. It is clear that the mode is the blue sports shoes as there are 3 pairs.




                                                       Sports shoes

The mode of a set of raw data is the observation that appears most often.



        Activity

Each time Vedesh walks to school from home, he observes the cars in a nearby parking
lot. One morning he decides to find the most popular type of car in the parking lot.




Can you help Vedesh find the most popular type of car? What is its colour?

In fact, the most popular type of car in this case is what we call the mode since it appears
most often in the given list of cars observed by Vedesh.



Example 1

    (a) 4, 13, 8, 11, 8, 3, 4 and 8.
                                                   

                                                                                                re-arrange the numbers in
                                                                                                ascending order so that it
Solution                                                                                        is easier to find mode.


    (a) Mode = 8            			                Since ‘8’ appears 3 times, i.e. most often, it is the mode.
